My mind jumps from whether we’ll hit the launch numbers, to whether someone on my team is about to quit, then to the mortgage or a client renewal. It feels like every small delay could turn into something major.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you feel anxious about things that aren't related to work or your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s usually tied to some responsibility, not random things out of nowhere. But it spreads beyond just work—like I’ll go from worrying about launch numbers to wondering if someone on my team is going to quit, then I’m thinking about the mortgage or whether a client will renew. It’s most days, and small delays start feeling like they could turn into something much bigger.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific objects, places, or situations outside of work that trigger your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not really specific objects or places, no. It’s more situations where I feel responsible for the outcome. Like my mind goes from whether we’ll hit our launch numbers, to if someone on my team might leave, then the mortgage or a client renewal. Even small delays start to feel like they could turn into something major.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about things outside of work and responsibilities most days?\n\nPatient: Yes, most days. It usually starts with work, but then it turns into other responsibilities too—like the mortgage or whether a client will renew. My mind just keeps jumping from one thing to the next, and even a small delay can feel like it’s going to become a big problem.\n\nDoctor: Is there any part of your day or any activity where you don't feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not really. Even when I’m home doing something normal, like eating dinner, I still feel wound up—like I’m still in a work review or waiting for something to go wrong. My shoulders stay tight a lot of the time, even when I’m trying to relax.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in your life that you do not worry about?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something, but not much comes to mind. Most days my mind jumps around—from whether we’ll hit launch numbers, to whether someone on my team might quit, then to the mortgage or a client renewal. Even little delays start feeling like they could turn into something much bigger.\n\nDoctor: Has your anxiety affected your relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s started affecting both work and home. At work, I spend so much time second-guessing decisions that pipeline reviews get tense, and I can tell it affects the team. Then by the time I get home, I’m just drained and more reactive than I want to be, so I’m not really fully present with my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious about these same things if you weren't in your current job or had different responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s just this job, honestly. The job gives me a lot to worry about, but my mind would probably still find things to latch onto. Like now it jumps from launch numbers to whether someone on my team might leave, then to the mortgage or a client renewal. Even small delays start feeling like they could become a much bigger problem.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Then I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning and I just can’t get my brain to shut off again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're lying awake at night thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights it turns into this anxious loop. I’ll start thinking about whether we’re going to hit the launch numbers, then I’m worried someone on my team might quit, then it jumps to the mortgage or a client renewal. It’s like every small delay feels like it could become a big problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical sensations, like tension or pain, when you're feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: Yes. I feel wound up a lot of the time, like my body is still in a stressful work meeting even when I’m just at home having dinner. It’s most days, and small delays start feeling like they could turn into something much bigger.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific objects, places, or situations outside of work that trigger your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not really specific objects or places, no. It’s more situations where I feel responsible for the outcome. Like my mind goes from whether we’ll hit our launch numbers, to if someone on my team might leave, then the mortgage or a client renewal. Even small delays start to feel like they could turn into something major.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about things outside of work and responsibilities most days?\n\nPatient: Yes, most days. It usually starts with work, but then it turns into other responsibilities too—like the mortgage or whether a client will renew. My mind just keeps jumping from one thing to the next, and even a small delay can feel like it’s going to become a big problem.\n\nDoctor: Is there any part of your day or any activity where you don't feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not really. Even when I’m home doing something normal, like eating dinner, I still feel wound up—like I’m still in a work review or waiting for something to go wrong. My shoulders stay tight a lot of the time, even when I’m trying to relax.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in your life that you do not worry about?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something, but not much comes to mind. Most days my mind jumps around—from whether we’ll hit launch numbers, to whether someone on my team might quit, then to the mortgage or a client renewal. Even little delays start feeling like they could turn into something much bigger.\n\nDoctor: Has your anxiety affected your relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s started affecting both work and home. At work, I spend so much time second-guessing decisions that pipeline reviews get tense, and I can tell it affects the team. Then by the time I get home, I’m just drained and more reactive than I want to be, so I’m not really fully present with my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious about these same things if you weren't in your current job or had different responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s just this job, honestly. The job gives me a lot to worry about, but my mind would probably still find things to latch onto. Like now it jumps from launch numbers to whether someone on my team might leave, then to the mortgage or a client renewal. Even small delays start feeling like they could become a much bigger problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any other physical symptoms, like rapid heartbeat or sweating, when you're feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about sweating so much, but I do feel really wound up physically. Like even when I’m home eating dinner, my body still feels like I’m sitting in a tense pipeline review. My shoulders stay tight a lot of the time, even when I’m trying to relax.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take a day off from work or avoid social events because of your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I haven’t usually taken full days off, but I have avoided some social things after work. By the time I get home I’m so drained and on edge that I don’t feel like I can really show up, even with my husband. And at work, it definitely affects me—like I spend too long second-guessing decisions, and then meetings can get tense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety is controlling your life or making it hard for you to do the things you need or want to do?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is starting to. It’s affecting both work and home. At work I spend so much time second-guessing decisions that things like pipeline reviews get tense, and then by the time I get home I’m just drained and more reactive than I want to be. I’m not really fully present with my husband, even when I want to be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. My shoulders especially stay really tight, even when I’m trying to relax.\n\nDoctor: Are you anxious all the time or is it mostly when you're thinking about work or specific problems?\n\nPatient: It’s most days, honestly. Work is the main thing it starts with, but it doesn’t stay there. My mind jumps from whether we’ll hit the launch numbers, to whether someone on my team is about to quit, then to the mortgage or a client renewal. It feels like every small delay could turn into something major.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you feel anxious about things that aren't related to work or your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s usually tied to some responsibility, not random things out of nowhere. But it spreads beyond just work—like I’ll go from worrying about launch numbers to wondering if someone on my team is going to quit, then I’m thinking about the mortgage or whether a client will renew. It’s most days, and small delays start feeling like they could turn into something much bigger.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific objects, places, or situations outside of work that trigger your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not really specific objects or places, no. It’s more situations where I feel responsible for the outcome. Like my mind goes from whether we’ll hit our launch numbers, to if someone on my team might leave, then the mortgage or a client renewal. Even small delays start to feel like they could turn into something major.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about things outside of work and responsibilities most days?\n\nPatient: Yes, most days. It usually starts with work, but then it turns into other responsibilities too—like the mortgage or whether a client will renew. My mind just keeps jumping from one thing to the next, and even a small delay can feel like it’s going to become a big problem.\n\nDoctor: Is there any part of your day or any activity where you don't feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not really. Even when I’m home doing something normal, like eating dinner, I still feel wound up—like I’m still in a work review or waiting for something to go wrong. My shoulders stay tight a lot of the time, even when I’m trying to relax.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in your life that you do not worry about?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something, but not much comes to mind. Most days my mind jumps around—from whether we’ll hit launch numbers, to whether someone on my team might quit, then to the mortgage or a client renewal. Even little delays start feeling like they could turn into something much bigger.\n\nDoctor: Has your anxiety affected your relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s started affecting both work and home. At work, I spend so much time second-guessing decisions that pipeline reviews get tense, and I can tell it affects the team. Then by the time I get home, I’m just drained and more reactive than I want to be, so I’m not really fully present with my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious about these same things if you weren't in your current job or had different responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s just this job, honestly. The job gives me a lot to worry about, but my mind would probably still find things to latch onto. Like now it jumps from launch numbers to whether someone on my team might leave, then to the mortgage or a client renewal. Even small delays start feeling like they could become a much bigger problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any other physical symptoms, like rapid heartbeat or sweating, when you're feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about sweating so much, but I do feel really wound up physically. Like even when I’m home eating dinner, my body still feels like I’m sitting in a tense pipeline review. My shoulders stay tight a lot of the time, even when I’m trying to relax.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take a day off from work or avoid social events because of your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I haven’t usually taken full days off, but I have avoided some social things after work. By the time I get home I’m so drained and on edge that I don’t feel like I can really show up, even with my husband. And at work, it definitely affects me—like I spend too long second-guessing decisions, and then meetings can get tense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety is controlling your life or making it hard for you to do the things you need or want to do?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is starting to. It’s affecting both work and home. At work I spend so much time second-guessing decisions that things like pipeline reviews get tense, and then by the time I get home I’m just drained and more reactive than I want to be. I’m not really fully present with my husband, even when I want to be.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that you do or any situation where you feel completely free from anxiety?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not really completely. There are moments where I’m distracted, but most days my mind jumps around—from whether we’ll hit the launch numbers, to whether someone on my team is about to quit, then the mortgage or a client renewal. It’s like every small delay starts feeling like it could turn into something major.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "role": "system",

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F45.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because my sleep has gotten really unreliable. Even when I’m exhausted and go to bed, I end up lying there thinking through work forecasts and team issues. Then I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning and I just can’t get my brain to shut off again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're lying awake at night thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights it turns into this anxious loop. I’ll start thinking about whether we’re going to hit the launch numbers, then I’m worried someone on my team might quit, then it jumps to the mortgage or a client renewal. It’s like every small delay feels like it could become a big problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical sensations, like tension or pain, when you're feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: Yes. I feel wound up a lot of the time, like my body is still in a stressful work meeting even when I’m just at home having dinner. My shoulders especially stay really tight, even when I’m trying to relax.\n\nDoctor: Are you anxious all the time or is it mostly when you're thinking about work or specific problems?\n\nPatient: It’s most days, honestly. Work is the main thing it starts with, but it doesn’t stay there. My mind jumps from whether we’ll hit the launch numbers, to whether someone on my team is about to quit, then to the mortgage or a client renewal. It feels like every small delay could turn into something major.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you feel anxious about things that aren't related to work or your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s usually tied to some responsibility, not random things out of nowhere. But it spreads beyond just work—like I’ll go from worrying about launch numbers to wondering if someone on my team is going to quit, then I’m thinking about the mortgage or whether a client will renew. It’s most days, and small delays start feeling like they could turn into something much bigger.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific objects, places, or situations outside of work that trigger your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not really specific objects or places, no. It’s more situations where I feel responsible for the outcome. Like my mind goes from whether we’ll hit our launch numbers, to if someone on my team might leave, then the mortgage or a client renewal. Even small delays start to feel like they could turn into something major.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about things outside of work and responsibilities most days?\n\nPatient: Yes, most days. It usually starts with work, but then it turns into other responsibilities too—like the mortgage or whether a client will renew. My mind just keeps jumping from one thing to the next, and even a small delay can feel like it’s going to become a big problem.\n\nDoctor: Is there any part of your day or any activity where you don't feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not really. Even when I’m home doing something normal, like eating dinner, I still feel wound up—like I’m still in a work review or waiting for something to go wrong. My shoulders stay tight a lot of the time, even when I’m trying to relax.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in your life that you do not worry about?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something, but not much comes to mind. Most days my mind jumps around—from whether we’ll hit launch numbers, to whether someone on my team might quit, then to the mortgage or a client renewal. Even little delays start feeling like they could turn into something much bigger.\n\nDoctor: Has your anxiety affected your relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s started affecting both work and home. At work, I spend so much time second-guessing decisions that pipeline reviews get tense, and I can tell it affects the team. Then by the time I get home, I’m just drained and more reactive than I want to be, so I’m not really fully present with my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious about these same things if you weren't in your current job or had different responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s just this job, honestly. The job gives me a lot to worry about, but my mind would probably still find things to latch onto. Like now it jumps from launch numbers to whether someone on my team might leave, then to the mortgage or a client renewal. Even small delays start feeling like they could become a much bigger problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any other physical symptoms, like rapid heartbeat or sweating, when you're feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about sweating so much, but I do feel really wound up physically. Like even when I’m home eating dinner, my body still feels like I’m sitting in a tense pipeline review. My shoulders stay tight a lot of the time, even when I’m trying to relax.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take a day off from work or avoid social events because of your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I haven’t usually taken full days off, but I have avoided some social things after work. By the time I get home I’m so drained and on edge that I don’t feel like I can really show up, even with my husband. And at work, it definitely affects me—like I spend too long second-guessing decisions, and then meetings can get tense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety is controlling your life or making it hard for you to do the things you need or want to do?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is starting to. It’s affecting both work and home. At work I spend so much time second-guessing decisions that things like pipeline reviews get tense, and then by the time I get home I’m just drained and more reactive than I want to be. I’m not really fully present with my husband, even when I want to be.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that you do or any situation where you feel completely free from anxiety?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not really completely. There are moments where I’m distracted, but most days my mind jumps around—from whether we’ll hit the launch numbers, to whether someone on my team is about to quit, then the mortgage or a client renewal. It’s like every small delay starts feeling like it could turn into something major.\n\nDoctor: Is there any aspect of your life where your anxiety seems less prominent or entirely absent?\n\nPatient: Not really. I’ll start thinking about whether we’re going to hit the launch numbers, then I’m worried someone on my team might quit, then it jumps to the mortgage or a client renewal.
